The ashes were placed in the eastern part of the famous cemetery of Père Lachaise, in a grave numbered 17616.
“Il y a un chemin vers le haut et un chemin vers le bas.
Le chemin d’en haut et le chemin d’en bas ne sont qu’une seule et même route” (“There is a road which leads upward and there is a road which leads downward.
Both are one and the same").
This was one of Eileen's notes.

This is an attempt to catalogue all of Eileen Gray's pieces.
It has been compiled from photographs and letters and from Eileens own descriptions.
It has been extremely difficult to come to a chronology: Eßleen herself was very vague about dates and never signed or dated anything—with the exception of no.
2 in this catalogue. signed at the client's request.
Often pieces were left unfinished, or finished much later Frequently pieces were altered at different times.
Many were considered prototypes.
Even when Eileen made the same piece several times, she often changed the design; few items are identical.
We have limited the descriptions to the most essential characteristies.
For more detailed descriptions, see the main text of the book.
Where pieces are lost, we have adopted the descriptions by Ealeen Gray herself.
Much information was gathered from the sales ledger of lean Désert. but this information is very sketchy.
In some-cases, the customer was mentioned, in others not.
Some items are hardly described at all.
Where the customer was known. we tried (in Eileen's time) to contact them bat usually failed to get any response.
Most customers had died, or their addresses were no longer applicable.
We have indicated the prices of the lost items in the hope that these can give some clues as to the importance of the various pieces.
We have listed only the freestanding furniture.
All of Eileen's houses had numerous shelves, headboards folding tables, ete.. built in. which were an integrated part of the arehitecture.
Information on these can be gathered from the pictures in the main part of the book.
Almost all of the butilt-in furniture was still in existence in E.
1027 when we last visited it, in 1979.
Since then, the present owner has taken the freestanding pieces to Switzerland.
In Tempe à Pailla. there was still the large extendable wardrobe.
The headboard remained in the rue Bonaparte bedroom.
There was no built-in furmiture in Lou Pérou.
The stodio in the rue Chateaubriand was gutted by various owners, who left only the built-in lamps.
They have now been removed.
In the case of the furniture designed for E.
1027.
Tempe à Pailla, rue Bonaparte, and Lou Pérou, the. owner was always Eileen Gray.
In the case of the rue Chateaubriand, the first owner was Jean Badovici or Eileen Gray.
In 1980 the most important sale of Eileen Gray's own furniture was conducted by Sotheby's in Monte Carlo.
Many of these pieces have changed hands since.
All objeets in private hands have been marked "Private collection." Eileen herself gave away a mumber of important pieces which are now in a private collection in London.
The most important exhibition since Eileen's death was that shown at the Vietoria and Albert Museum in London, in 1979, at the Scottish Arts Council Gallery in Edinburgh, in 1979, and at The Museum of Modern Art in New York, in 1980.
The exhibition of the Royal Institute of British Architeets at the Heinz Gallery in London, in 1972, showed only a few pieces of furniture The most important sourees of photographie material were E.
1027 Maison en Bord de Mer, Wendingen, Sotheby's sale catalogue, Monaco, 1980, and Eileen Gray's own photograph archives.
There have been a number of pirated copies of Eilcen Gray pieces.
Ouly two firms have been granted the right to reproduce Eileen Gray furniture.
Aram Design Ltd., London. directed by Zeff Aram, was authorized by Eileen Gray herself: the other one, Ecart, Paris, directed by Andrée Putman. was authorized by the heirs to Eileen Gray.
Eileen left a cousiderable mumber of rug designs in various states of completion.
The whole area of rugs. rug designs, and graphie works on paper is extremely complex, and not enough information is available to treat them in depth in this catalogue.
A special study will have to be made to ascertain which rugs were executed.
The catalogue is arranged as follows Screens 1-20 Panels, Doors, and Friezes–21–31 Sofas, Divans, and Beds–32–45 Chairs, Stools, Sofas, and Benches 46-74 Tables75–130 Bookcases, Cupboards, and Chests131-42 Lamps 143–78 Mirrors 179-93 Miscellancous– 194-212

## (continued
from front flap) In her early days at London's Slade School of Art and in Paris, Eileen met many artists, writers, and architects.
The American expatriate writers Gertrude Stein and Natalie Barney were admirers of her work.
Somerset Maugham, Wyndham Lewis, and the "black magician” Aleister Crowley were among her friends and suitors.
Her striking beauty. captured by Berenice Abbott in memorable photographs, led her into other tumultuous relationships, notably with the architect Jean Badovici, for whom she designed a bachelor apartment and for whom she built the first of her two houses. –At the end of World War II. she found the houses she had designed vandalized, and her furniture, her sketchbooks and plans destroyed.
With the energy of a person half her age, she resumed designing projects–for a prefabricated cultural center and a vacation complex—and built a new home in the south of France.
But her international fame had faded, and she spent her last years in solitude, almost forgotten. –It is Peter Adams achievement in this engrossing biography to recreate the worlds Eileen lived in, from the fin de siècle to the late twentieth century, from the convivial times shared with her famous—and eceentrie—friends in the Art Deco period to the stark. lonely years spent working out plans for pioneering modern houses.
Adam traces the evolution of her theories in drawings and important articles, translating and reproducing much work never published before or largely inaccessible.
He also provides a catalogue raisonné of all her known furniture–a necessity for collectors and lovers of Art Deco at its nest. –Peter Adamn has won many awards for his BBC-TV films on Richard Strauss, David Hockney.

10011 Printed in Japan “Eileen Gray is a very special, unique figure in the world of new form-finding, . . .
The choice of the material and the way in which it is used do justice to these forms. . . .
She is the kind of artist of whom there are very few in existence —Jan Wils, De Stijl architect, 1924 “Eileen Gray occupies the center of the modern movement . . . she rejects the feeling of the old aesthetics and mistrusts old forms. . . .
The formidable influence of technology has transformed our sensibilities. . . .
All her work reflects a lyrical force, an enthusiasm, and the strength of feeling of this new civilization and spirit. . . . —Jean Badovici, editor of LArchitecture Vivante, 1924 “I am so happy to tell you how much those few days spent in your house have made me appreciate the rare spirit which has given the modern furniture and installations such a dignified, charming, and witty sha —Le Corbusier, in a letter to Eileen Gray, 1938 What the “. . . her furnishings, designs and architectural studies were prophetie in concept, and the mark left by her talent is scernible to this day —Monica Geran, Interior Design, 1980 "Gray's style is surprising, intriguing and entirely her —Ronny H.
Cohen, Art in America, 1980 “. . .
Eileen Gray . . . ranks among the most important designers of this century. —Martin Fille, The New York Times Magazine, 1980
